An order-theoretic circuit syntax and characterisation of the concept lattice
Tein van der Lugt

Abstract
We take an order-theoretic approach to circuit (string diagram) syntax, treating a circuit as a partial order with additional input-output structure. We define morphisms between circuits and prove a factorisation theorem showing that these can, in the finite case, be regarded as formalising a notion of syntactical circuit rewrites, with quotient maps in particular corresponding to gate composition. We then consider the connectivity of a circuit, expressed as a binary relation between its inputs and outputs, and characterise the concept lattice from formal concept analysis as the unique smallest circuit that admits morphisms from all other circuits with the same connectivity. This has significance for quantum causality, particularly to the study of causal decompositions of unitary transformations. We close by constructing the circuit characterised by the dual statement.



\DeclareSortingNamekeyTemplate
\keypart
\keypart
\keypart
\keypart

1
Introduction

This paper is about the syntax of
circuits
, or
string diagrams
, which describe parallel and sequential compositions of processes in symmetric monoidal categories (SMCs).
A particular motivating application for this work, however, lies in causal inference and quantum foundations, where classical or quantum circuits may act as models of causal processes.
In this context, the syntax of a circuit—i.e. its ‘shape’—alone implies relevant information about the process (morphism of the SMC) it implements: for example, the absence of a path between an input and an output of the circuit prohibits the flow of causal influence between those systems.
Generally, one may wonder about the full class of morphisms in a specified SMC—be it classical probability theory, quantum theory, or any other—that can be expressed by a circuit with a given shape, a type of problem sometimes referred to as
causal compatibility
.
1
1

1

See e.g.
[
1
]
. In quantum and classical causal modelling, causal structures are often represented by directed acyclic graphs; however, string diagrams may equally well be used
[
2
]
.

A circuit shape
Q
may then be considered ‘at least as expressive’ as a shape
P
if it can express at least as many morphisms of the SMC.
Sometimes, this is the case simply because any circuit with shape
P
can be rewritten into one with shape
Q
using purely syntactical operations valid in any SMC, such as composing two or more gates, treating them as a single one; or adding trivial gates and trivial wires.

Here, we propose an order-theoretic treatment of circuit syntax and such syntactical rewrites.
We then consider the input-output
connectivity
of a circuit and characterise the
concept lattice

[
3
,
4
,
5
]
as the most expressive circuit with a given connectivity.
This result has particular relevance to the search for
causal decompositions
in quantum theory
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
.
These are circuit decompositions of a unitary transformation where certain no-influence conditions satisfied by the unitary are reflected in the connectivity of the circuit.
While the general existence of such circuit decompositions remains an open problem, the present result demonstrates the existence of a canonical shape for those that do exist—the concept lattice—thereby answering a question posed in
[
6
]
.
In upcoming work
[
7
]
this syntactic result is combined with a semantic (operator-algebraic) proof to construct causal decompositions and characterise precisely when a particular type of them exists.

This paper is structured as follows.
In
Section

2
, we propose a definition of a
circuit
as a partial order
P
supplemented by two maps
{\lambda:A\to P}
,
\mu:B\to P
into it.
We also introduce the relevant structure-preserving maps, which we call
circuit morphisms
.
In case
A
,
B
, and
P
are finite, the elements of
P
can be thought of as gates in a circuit diagram, the elements of
A
and
B
as the overall input and output wires, and
\lambda,\mu
as providing the locations of these wires in the circuit diagram.
However, all results in this work, with the exception of
Proposition

14
, hold in the infinite case, too.

Section

3
examines the structure of circuit morphisms.
We recall the theory of quotients of partially ordered sets by
compatible congruences
studied in
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
and show in
Theorem

20
that each circuit morphism factorises into a quotient map and a number of other elementary types of morphisms.
For the finite case, this section serves to show that circuit morphisms capture an appropriate notion of syntactical circuit rewrites, with quotient maps in particular corresponding to gate composition.

Finally,
Section

4
focusses on the
connectivity
of a circuit, which is a binary relation
G\subseteq A\times B
specifying which inputs can reach which outputs via directed paths through the circuit.
We recall from
[
3
]
that out of any given binary relation
G\subseteq A\times B
one may construct a complete lattice, called the
concept lattice
in formal concept analysis
[
4
,
5
]
; supplemented with the natural choice of maps
\lambda,\mu
into it, it defines a circuit
{L_{G}}
.
Our main result,
Theorem

29
, is a characterisation of
{L_{G}}
as the smallest circuit with connectivity
G
that admits morphisms from all other circuits with the same connectivity.
In the sense described above, this shows in particular that
{L_{G}}
is most expressive, in terms of SMC morphism expressibility, among all circuits with connectivity
G
.

2
First definitions

A preorder on a set
P
is a relation
\leq\,\subseteq P\times P
that is (i)
reflexive
:
\forall p\in P:p\leq p
and (ii)
transitive
:
\forall p,q,r\in P:p\leq q\land q\leq r\implies p\leq r
; a (partial) order is one that is also (iii)
antisymmetric
:
\forall p,q\in P:p\leq q\land q\leq p\implies p=q
.
We write
p<q
iff
p\leq q
and
p\neq q
;
p\mathrel{\#}q
iff
p\nleq q
and
q\nleq p
; and
p\lessdot q
iff
p

covers

q
from below, that is, if
p<q
and there is no
r\in P
so that
p<r<q
.

Definition 1
.

Let
A
and
B
be sets.
A
circuit
from
A
to
B
is a tuple
(P,\leq,\lambda,\mu)
of a set
P
, a partial order
\leq
on
P
, and two functions
\lambda:A\to P
and
\mu:B\to P
called the
input map
and
output map
, respectively.
We will often refer to elements of
P
as
gates
and to elements of
A
and
B
as
inputs
and
outputs
, respectively.

P
may also refer to a circuit itself, rather than only its underlying set; in this case we write
\leq
or
\leq_{P}
and
\lambda_{P},\mu_{P}
for the order and maps constituting the circuit on
P
.

Definition 2
.

We call a circuit
P

finite
if the set
P
as well as the sets
A
and
B
are.
Finite circuits may be conveniently depicted by diagrams.
First of all, the
Hasse diagram
of the partial order
(P,\leq)
is the directed acyclic graph on
P
obtained by drawing an edge from
p
to
q
iff
p\lessdot q
.
The elements of
P
are laid out on the page so that all edges point upward; the latter are then drawn as undirected edges.
The
circuit diagram
of
(P,\leq,\lambda,\mu)
is obtained by supplementing the Hasse diagram with nodes for each
a\in A
and
b\in B
and upward-directed edges from
a
to
\lambda(a)
and from
\mu(b)
to
b
.
We will often leave the gates of the circuit unlabelled, in which case the diagram determines the circuit up to circuit isomorphisms (defined imminently).

Example 3
.

The diagram

q
p
r
s
a_{3}
b_{1}
b_{2}
a_{2}
a_{1}

denotes the circuit
(P,\leq,\lambda,\mu)
where
P=\{p,q,r,s\}
;
\leq
is the unique partial order satisfying
q<p
,
q<r<s
,
p\mathrel{\#}r
and
p\mathrel{\#}s
;
the sets of inputs and outputs are
A=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
and
B=\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
; and, finally, the input map
\lambda:A\to P
acts as
a_{1}\mapsto p,a_{2}\mapsto r,a_{3}\mapsto s
and the output map
\mu:B\to P
as
b_{1}\mapsto p
and
b_{2}\mapsto s
.
2
2

2

Other treatments of circuits and string diagram syntax are based on directed (acyclic) graphs, or similar structures, from the outset (see e.g.
[
14
]
). Such an approach allows for a distinction between circuits with identical transitive reductions, i.e. that induce the same partial order among their gates. In
Example

3
, for instance, one could allow for an additional wire directly from gate
q
to gate
s
. Our order-based formalism here does not make such distinctions. They are not relevant to the question of expressivity described in the Introduction, since the wire from
q
to
s
can just as well be incorporated into the gate
r
.

Definition 4
.

Let
P,Q
be circuits from
A
to
B
.
We call a map
f:P\to Q
a
(circuit) morphism
if it satisfies the following properties.

(i)

f
is
order-preserving
: for all
p,p^{\prime}\in P
,
p\leq p^{\prime}\implies f(p)\leq f(p^{\prime})
.

(ii)

f

respects inputs
: for all
a\in A
,
f(\lambda_{P}(a))\geq\lambda_{Q}(a)
.

(iii)

f

respects outputs
: for all
b\in B
,
f(\mu_{P}(b))\leq\mu_{Q}(b)
.

Moreover, we say
f
is a
(circuit) embedding
, and write
f:P\hookrightarrow Q
, if it is a circuit morphism and an order embedding (i.e.
f(p)\leq f(p^{\prime})\implies p\leq p^{\prime}
).
A
(circuit) isomorphism

f:P\xrightarrow{\sim}Q
is a bijective circuit morphism whose inverse is also a circuit morphism.

Remark
.

We regard the input and output sets
A
and
B
as (arbitrary but) fixed; two circuits can be isomorphic only if they are defined on identical input and output sets.
Moreover, two circuits that differ only by a permutation on
A
or
B
may not be isomorphic.

Furthermore, if
f:P\to Q
is bijective and a circuit embedding, then it is automatically an
order
isomorphism (that is, an order-preserving map with order-preserving inverse); however, its inverse
f^{-1}
is only a circuit morphism—and
f
therefore only a
circuit
isomorphism—if it also satisfies the
equalities

f\circ\lambda_{P}=\lambda_{Q}
and
f\circ\mu_{P}=\mu_{Q}
.
The map
f
illustrated below is an example of a bijective circuit embedding that fails this property: it satisfies
f(\mu_{P}(b_{1}))<\mu_{Q}(b_{1})
, so its inverse is not a circuit morphism.

(1)

We will often be interested not so much in the properties of a particular morphism
P\to Q
, but rather in whether such a morphism exists in the first place.
Since circuit morphisms can be regarded as syntatical rewriting procedures (as we will argue in the next section), the existence of such a morphism indicates that
Q
is at least as expressive as
P
, in the sense described in the Introduction.
Therefore we define the following.

Definition 5
.

Denote by
\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}
the class of circuits from
A
to
B
preordered by the relation
\preceq
so that
P\preceq Q
iff there exists a morphism
P\to Q
.
When both
P\preceq Q
and
Q\preceq P
, we will say that
P
and
Q
are
syntactically equivalent
.

Syntactical equivalence of
P
and
Q
is strictly weaker than the existence of a circuit isomorphism between
P
and
Q
; see for instance
Example

9
below.
First, note the following, which is useful for showing syntactical
in
equivalence.

Definition 6
.

Let
(P,\leq,\lambda,\mu)
be a circuit.
For
p\in P
, we write

p^{-}\coloneqq\{a\in A:\lambda(a)\leq p\}\text{\hskip 20.44434ptand\hskip 20.44434pt}p^{+}\coloneqq\{b\in B:\mu(b)\geq p\}.

(2)

That is,
p^{-}
are the inputs that lie to
p
’s past,
p^{+}
the outputs lying to
p
’s future.

Proposition 7
.

If
f:P\to Q
is a circuit morphism, then for any
p\in P
,
p^{-}\subseteq f(p)^{-}
and
p^{+}\subseteq f(p)^{+}
.

Proof.

Suppose
a\in p^{-}
: that is,
\lambda_{P}(a)\leq p
.
Since
f
is a circuit morphism, we have
\lambda_{Q}(a)\leq f(\lambda_{P}(a))\leq f(p)
.
Therefore
a\in f(p)^{-}
.
The inclusion
p^{+}\subseteq f(p)^{+}
follows similarly.
∎

Example 8
.

A=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
B=\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
and consider the circuits

\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

The map
f:P\to Q
that sends all four elements
a,b,c,d
of
P
to
q\in Q
is clearly a circuit morphism.
However, there is no circuit morphism from
Q
to
P
.
Note that
Q
has a gate
q
(its only element) satisfying
q^{-}=A
and
q^{+}=B
.
By
Proposition

7
, having such a gate is a property of
Q
that must be preserved by circuit morphisms
Q\to P
; however,
P
does not have any such gate.

P
and
Q
are therefore strictly ordered as
P\prec Q
.
3
3

3

These two circuits have particular significance to classical and quantum causation.
In the ‘classical’ SMC of stochastic maps,
P
and
Q
are equally expressive: any stochastic map between sets
a_{1}\times a_{2}
and
b_{1}\times b_{2}
can be expressed as the composition of four stochastic maps as in
P
.
This relies on the ability to clone classical information and underlies the procedure of
exogenisaton
of latent variables in classical causal reasoning
[
15
]
.
In quantum theory, however, where information cannot be cloned, there are bipartite quantum channels that rely on an irreducible type of
interaction
between all four systems
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
which cannot be simulated by circuits of the form
P
(see e.g.
[
16
]
).
This
quantum
inequivalence of
P
and
Q
, which has consequences for cryptography
[
17
]
and holography
[
18
]
, thus reflects the
syntactical
inequivalence witnessed by the inexistence of a morphism
Q\to P
.

Example 9
.

The two circuits in
Eq.

1
are not isomorphic but they do admit morphisms in both directions.
(The morphism
f
in
Eq.

1
provides one from left to right; a morphism in the other direction is given by mapping both elements of the circuit on the right to the bottom element of the circuit on the left.)
All these three circuits are therefore syntactically equivalent yet not isomorphic.

3
Circuit morphisms

The main purpose of this section, which is independent of our main result in
Section

4
, is to show that in the case of finite circuits, circuit morphisms provide a natural notion of syntactical circuit rewrites.
We do this by showing in
Theorem

20
that any morphism factorises into morphisms of particular elementary types, all of which can clearly be interpreted as valid syntactical rewrites in the finite case.
However, we refrain from making a formal connection with symmetric monoidal categories, keeping the focus instead on the order theory, and all notions and results, with the exception of
Proposition

14
, are defined and hold in the infinite case as well.
We start with some background on quotients of partial orders
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
.

Definition 10
.

Let
(P,\leq)
be a partial order and
\theta
an equivalence relation on
P
.
Denote by
[p]
the equivalence class of
p\in P
under
\theta
and define the binary relation
\leq_{\theta}
on the quotient set
P/\theta
by

[p]\leq_{\theta}[q]\quad:\!\iff\quad\exists p^{\prime}\in[p],q^{\prime}\in[q]\text{ such that }p^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}.

(3)

In other words, it is the minimal relation on
P/\theta
making the quotient map
\pi_{\theta}:P\to P/\theta,p\mapsto[p]
order-preserving.

While the relation
\leq_{\theta}
so defined is always reflexive, it may not be antisymmetric or transitive, and thus not necessarily a partial order.

Example 11
.

If
P=\{p,q,r\}
,
p<q<r
, and the equivalence classes of
\theta
are
\{p,r\}
and
\{q\}
, then
\leq_{\theta}
is not antisymmetric, as we have
\{p,r\}\leq_{\theta}\{q\}\leq_{\theta}\{p,r\}
.
Moreover, if
P=\{p,q,r,s\}
,
p<q
,
r<s
, and
\{p,q\}
and
\{r,s\}
are
\leq
-incomparable, then the order
\leq_{\theta}
defined by the equivalence relation
\theta
with equivalence classes
\{p\},\{r,s\},\{q\}
is not transitive.

The following notion has been studied in detail in Refs.
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
, amongst other works.

Definition 12
.

An equivalence relation
\theta
on
(P,\leq)
is a
compatible congruence
4
4

4

We take this terminology from
[
13
,
19
]
; this has also simply been called an
order congruence

[
12
]
.

if the transitive closure
\overrightarrow{\leq_{\theta}}
of
\leq_{\theta}
is antisymmetric and hence a partial order.
When
\theta
is a compatible congruence, we will henceforth equip the set
P/\theta
with the order
\overrightarrow{\leq_{\theta}}
.
Moreover, when
{(P,\leq,\lambda,\mu)}
is a circuit, we denote by
P/\theta
the circuit
(P/\theta,\overrightarrow{\leq_{\theta}},\pi_{\theta}\circ\lambda,\pi_{\theta}\circ\mu)
, where
{\pi_{\theta}:P\to P/\theta}
is the quotient map.

Which equivalences
\theta
are compatible congruences?
First, call a subset
S
of
P

convex
if for all
s,t\in S
and
p\in P
,
s\leq p\leq t\implies p\in S
.
If
\theta
is a compatible congruence on
P
, then every equivalence class
[p]
is necessarily convex
[
9
, §36]
; this is required to avoid failure of antisymmetry of
\leq_{\theta}
(cf.
Example

11
).
However, not every equivalence relation
\theta
on
P
whose equivalence classes are convex is a compatible congruence.
As an example, consider the order underlying the circuit
P
from
Example

8
and the equivalence relation
\theta
whose equivalence classes are
\{a,d\}
and
\{b,c\}
.
Once one identifies
a
and
d
, the equivalence class
\{b,c\}
becomes non-convex, resulting in a relation
\leq_{\theta}
that is not antisymmetric.
However, as long as one performs a sequence of quotients that identify the elements of a
single
convex subset at a time, the resulting relation will remain a partial order.
Every quotient by a compatible congruence in fact arises in this way.

Definition 13
.

Let
S\subseteq P
be a convex set.
Denote by
\theta_{S}
the equivalence relation on
P
that has equivalence classes
S
and
\{p\}
for
p\in P\setminus S
.
If
S
contains precisely two elements, that is,
S=\{p,q\}
where
p\lessdot q
or
p\mathrel{\#}q
, then we call
\theta_{S}
an
atomic compatible congruence
.

Proposition 14
.

If
S\subseteq P
is convex, then
\theta_{S}
is a compatible congruence.
Moreover, if
P
is finite then an equivalence relation
\theta
on
P
is a compatible congruence if and only if the quotient map
\pi_{\theta}:P\to P/\theta
is, up to order isomorphisms, a finite composition of quotients by atomic compatible congruences.

We omit the (straightforward) proof (see also
[
10
, §7]
), the main point being that each quotient by a compatible congruence can be regarded as the result of a ‘gate composition’ procedure in a circuit diagram, with each atomic compatible congruence
\theta_{\{p,q\}}
corresponding to a single sequential composition (if
p\lessdot q
or
q\lessdot p
) or a single parallel composition (monoidal product; if
p\mathrel{\#}q
).
The connection with circuit morphisms is due to the following.

Definition 15
.

The
kernel
of a map
f:P\to Q
, denoted
\ker f
, is the equivalence relation on
P
whose equivalence classes are the fibres of
f
—that is,
p(\ker f)p^{\prime}\iff f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
for
p,p^{\prime}\in P
.

Proposition 16

(
[
11
, §2]
,
[
13
, Prop. 4.10]
)
.

An equivalence
\theta
on
P
is a compatible congruence if and only if it is the kernel of an order-preserving map
f:P\to Q
for some partially ordered set
Q
.

Proposition 17
.

Let
f:P\to Q
be a circuit morphism.
Let
\pi_{f}:P\twoheadrightarrow P/\ker f
be the quotient map and
[f]:P/\ker f\to Q
the (well-defined) injective map
[p]\mapsto f(p)
.
Then
\pi_{f}
and
[f]
are circuit morphisms and
f=[f]\circ\pi_{f}
:

(4)

Proof.

The only nontrivial statement to verify is that
[f]:P/\ker f\to Q
is a circuit morphism.
Recall that
P/\ker f
is ordered by
\overrightarrow{\leq_{\ker f}}
and has input and output maps
\lambda_{P/\ker f}=\pi_{f}\circ\lambda_{P}
and
\mu_{P/\ker f}=\pi_{f}\circ\mu_{P}
, respectively.
To show that
[f]
is order-preserving, first suppose that
[p],[q]\in P/\ker f
and
[p]\leq_{\ker f}[q]
.
This means that there exist
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in P
so that
[p]=[p^{\prime}]
,
p^{\prime}\leq_{P}q^{\prime}
, and
[q^{\prime}]=[q]
.
By construction, then,

[f]([p])=f(p)=f(p^{\prime})\leq_{Q}f(q^{\prime})=f(q)=[f]([q]).

Thus,
[p]\leq_{\ker f}[q]\implies[f]([p])\leq_{Q}[f]([q])
.
Since
\leq_{Q}
is transitive, we also have
[p]\mathrel{\overrightarrow{\leq_{\ker f}}}[q]\implies[f]([p])\leq_{Q}[f]([q])
, so
[f]
is order-preserving.
That it also respects inputs is immediate:
[f]\circ\lambda_{P/\ker f}=[f]\circ\pi_{f}\circ\lambda_{P}=f\circ\lambda_{P}\geq\lambda_{Q}
pointwise for all
a\in A
, where the last inequality follows from the fact that
f
is itself a circuit morphism.
Similarly,
[f]
respects outputs.
∎

It remains to argue that in addition to the quotient map
\pi_{f}
, the injective circuit morphism
[f]
also models a valid syntactical circuit rewrite.
5
5

5

Note that in disanalogy to the isomorphism theorems for algebraic structures, the map
[f]:P/\ker f\to\im(f)\subseteq Q
is not necessarily a circuit isomorphism; it is injective but not always an order embedding.

Definition 18
.

The following are three types of injective circuit morphisms.
Each corresponds to a certain elementary type of circuit rewrite and is exemplified below.

(i)

Let
(P,\leq_{P},\lambda,\mu)
be a circuit from
A
to
B
and
S
an arbitrary set. Write
P\sqcup S
for the disjoint union of the sets
P
and
S
.
Let
\leq
be the partial order on
P\sqcup S
that satisfies
\leq\cap\,(P\times P)=\,\leq_{P}
and
s\mathrel{\#}q
for any
s\in S
and
q\in P\sqcup S
.
Denote by
P\sqcup S
the circuit from
A
to
B
given by
(P\sqcup S,\leq,\lambda,\mu)
.
Say that a circuit morphism
f:P\to Q

adds isolated gates
if
Q=P\sqcup S
for some set
S
and
f
is the inclusion map
P\hookrightarrow P\sqcup S
.
Each such map is in fact a circuit embedding.

(ii)

A circuit morphism that
adds wires
is one of the form
\id_{P}:(P,{\leq},\lambda,\mu)\to(P,{\leq^{\prime}},\lambda,\mu)
,
p\mapsto p
, where
\leq,\leq^{\prime}
are such that
p\leq q\implies p\leq^{\prime}q
.

(iii)

A circuit morphism that
advances inputs and delays outputs
is one of the form
\id_{P}:(P,{\leq},\lambda,\mu)\to(P,{\leq},\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
,
p\mapsto p
where
\lambda(a)\geq\lambda^{\prime}(a)
for all
a\in A
and
\mu(b)\leq\mu^{\prime}(b)
for all
b\in B
.
This is always an embedding.

Example 19
.

The first morphism below is a quotient map; the other three are of the types just introduced.

Theorem 20
.

A map
f:P\to Q
is a circuit morphism if and only if it is, up to isomorphisms, the composition of a finite sequence of maps of the following four types.

•

quotients by compatible congruences (which, in case
P
is finite, are in turn always finite compositions of quotients by atomic compatible congruences, by
Proposition

14
);

•

morphisms that add isolated gates;

•

morphisms that add wires;

•

morphisms that advance inputs and delay outputs.

Proof.

Let
f
be a circuit morphism.
By
Proposition

17
it suffices to show that
[f]:P/\ker f\to Q
factorises into morphisms of the latter three types.
Denote by
\tilde{Q}
the circuit
(Q,\leq_{Q},f\circ\lambda_{P},f\circ\mu_{P})
and consider

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi_{f}:P&\twoheadrightarrow P/\ker f\\
p&\mapsto[p];\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\id_{P/\ker f}:P/\ker f&\hookrightarrow P/\ker f\sqcup(Q\setminus\im(f))\\
\quad[p]&\mapsto[p];\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}g:P/\ker f\sqcup(Q\setminus\im(f))&\to\tilde{Q}\\
[p]&\mapsto[f]([p])\quad\text{ for }p\in P;\\
q&\mapsto q\quad\text{ for }q\in Q\setminus\im(f);\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\id_{Q}:\tilde{Q}&\hookrightarrow Q\\
q&\mapsto q.\end{split}

Recall from
Definition

18
that we consider
Q\setminus\im(f)
as discretely ordered.
It is clear that all four maps are, up to isomorphism, morphisms of the required types and that
f
factorises as

Remark
.

In addition to syntactical circuit rewrites, the morphisms from
Definition

4
can also be used to model implementations of circuits in a spacetime.
Let
(M,g)
be a time-oriented Lorentzian manifold without closed causal curves, so that the order
\preceq_{g}
on
M
induced by the metric
g
and the time orientation is a partial order.
Let
A
and
B
be finite sets of physical systems.
Functions
\lambda_{M}:A\to M
and
\mu_{M}:B\to M
provide localisations of these systems at points in spacetime, and together with the spacetime constitute a tuple
(M,\preceq_{g},\lambda_{M},\mu_{M})
which in our terminology is just a special ‘circuit’, in this case with infinite underlying set
M
.
A morphism
f:P\to M
from a finite circuit
P
into
M
provides a localisation of each of
P
’s gates at points in spacetime in such a way that appropriate causal curves are guaranteed to exist for each
P
’s wires (edges of its circuit diagram) and inputs and outputs are picked up and dropped off at the locations requested by
\lambda_{M}
and
\mu_{M}
.
See also Refs.
[
20
,
21
]
.

4
Connectivity

The following notion arises in quantum causality, in particular in the study of causal decompositions of unitary transformations
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
.

Definition 21
.

Let
P
be a circuit from
A
to
B
.
The
connectivity
of
P
is the binary relation
G_{P}\subseteq A\times B
satisfying

\forall a\in A,b\in B:\quad aG_{P}b\iff\lambda_{P}(a)\leq\mu_{P}(b).

(5)

This defines a map
G_{(-)}:\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}\to\mathcal{P}(A\times B),P\mapsto G_{P}
.
When the powerset
\mathcal{P}(A\times B)
is ordered under inclusion, this map is order-preserving.

This last claim is simply to verify.
It tells us that, as should be expected, syntactical circuit rewrites never decrease connectivity.
As a consequence, if two circuits have distinct connectivity, this immediately indicates that they are syntactically inequivalent (see
Definition

5
).
Also circuits with identical connectivity may however be inequivalent:

Example 22
.

If
C_{3}\subseteq A\times B
is the relation between
A\coloneqq\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
and
B\coloneqq\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\}
given by

There is no circuit morphism in the other direction, however; therefore
{B_{C_{3}}}\prec{L_{C_{3}}}
.
This follows by an application of
Proposition

7
similar to that in
Example

8
.
Indeed, there exist quantum channels that may be expressed by circuits of the form
{L_{C_{3}}}
but not by ones of the form
{B_{C_{3}}}
(cf.
Footnote

3
).

In
Section

4.1
, we construct the analogue of
{L_{C_{3}}}
for general relations
G\subseteq A\times B
and show that it in fact admits incoming morphisms from
all
other circuits with connectivity
G
.
We generalise
{B_{C_{3}}}
in
Section

4.2
, constructing a circuit
{B_{G}}
which satisfies the dual property, admitting outgoing morphisms into any other circuit with connectivity
G
.

4.1
The concept lattice

Let
A
and
B
be arbitrary sets.
From each binary relation
G\subseteq A\times B
one may construct a complete lattice via a construction first realised by
[
3
]
and extensively studied in formal concept analysis
[
4
,
5
]
.
We use the terminology from
[
4
,
5
]
and call this the
concept lattice
.
We will briefly recall this construction and then show, in
Theorem

29
, that with the natural choice of input and output maps, it constitutes the smallest circuit with connectivity
G
that admits incoming morphisms from every other circuit with the same connectivity.

We will use the notation

G(a)\coloneqq\{b\in B\mid aGb\}\text{\quad for }a\in A\text{\quad and\quad}G^{-1}(b)\coloneqq\{a\in A\mid aGb\}\text{\quad for }b\in B.

(6)

Denote by
\mathcal{P}(A)
and
\mathcal{P}(B)
the powersets of
A
and
B
and consider the maps
7
7

7

Our choice of letters
\mathfrak{c}
and
\mathfrak{p}
reflects the origins of this work in quantum causal modelling, where
G
encodes
causal structure
and the elements of
G(a)=\mathfrak{c}(\{a\})
are referred to as the ‘(causal) children’ of
a\in A
, while those of
G^{-1}(b)=\mathfrak{p}(\{b\})
are the ‘(causal) parents’ of
b\in B
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}(A)\to\mathcal{P}(B)\dblcolon\alpha\mapsto\bigcap_{a\in\alpha}G(a)=\{b\in B\mid\forall a\in\alpha:aGb\}

(7)

\displaystyle\text{and\quad}\mathfrak{p}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}(B)\to\mathcal{P}(A)\dblcolon\beta\mapsto\bigcap_{b\in\beta}G^{-1}(b)=\{a\in A\mid\forall b\in\beta:aGb\}.

(8)

In particular,
\mathfrak{c}(\emptyset)=B
and
\mathfrak{p}(\emptyset)=A
.
With
\mathcal{P}(A)
and
\mathcal{P}(B)
ordered under inclusion, these maps are order-reversing:

\begin{split}\forall\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\subseteq A&:\alpha\subseteq\alpha^{\prime}\implies\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)\supseteq\mathfrak{c}(\alpha^{\prime})\\
\text{ and \quad}\forall\beta,\beta^{\prime}\subseteq B&:\beta\subseteq\beta^{\prime}\implies\mathfrak{p}(\beta)\supseteq\mathfrak{p}(\beta^{\prime}).\end{split}

(9)

They also satisfy

\forall\alpha\subseteq A:\alpha\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall\beta\subseteq B:\beta\subseteq\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}(\beta).

(10)

Eqs.

9
and
10
make the pair
(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{p})
into an (antitone)
Galois connection
between the partially ordered sets
\mathcal{P}(A)
and
\mathcal{P}(B)

[
22
]
.
8
8

8

In categorical terms, these maps form a dual adjunction between the orders
\mathcal{P}(A)
and
\mathcal{P}(B)
seen as categories.

A direct consequence is that

\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}.

(11)

Moreover, the map
\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{P}(A)\to\mathcal{P}(A)
acts as a
closure operator
on
\mathcal{P}(A)
, meaning it is

(i)

extensive:
\forall\alpha\subseteq A:\alpha\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)
;

(ii)

idempotent:
\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}
; and

(iii)

order-preserving:
\forall\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\subseteq A:\alpha\subseteq\alpha^{\prime}\implies\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\alpha^{\prime})
.

We therefore say that
\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)
is the
closure
of
\alpha\subseteq A
and that
\alpha
is
closed
if
\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)=\alpha
.
It follows from (
i
)–(
iii
) that the closure of a set is precisely the smallest closed set containing it and that the intersection of (arbitrarily many) closed sets is again closed.
Moreover, by virtue of
Eq.

11
, the closed subsets of
A
are precisely the sets
\alpha
that lie in the image of
\mathfrak{p}
.

Example 23
.

With respect to the relation
C_{3}
defined in
Example

22
, the closure of
\{a_{1}\}\subseteq A
is
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
, while
\{a_{2}\}
is itself closed.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(A)
be the set of closed subsets of
A
.
As
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
is closed under arbitrary intersections, it forms a complete lattice when ordered under inclusion: that is, any subset of elements
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
has a greatest lower bound and a least upper bound in
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
.
They are given, respectively, by

\bigwedge_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}=\bigcap_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigvee_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}=\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}\right).

Dually, the map
\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{P}(B)\to\mathcal{P}(B)
satisfies conditions analogous to (
i
)–(
iii
) above and is therefore a closure operator on
\mathcal{P}(B)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)
be the set of closed subsets of
B
with respect to this closure operator.
Just like
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
,
(\mathcal{P}^{c}(B),\subseteq)
forms a complete lattice; so does the opposite order
\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}\coloneqq(\mathcal{P}^{c}(B),\supseteq)
.
Note that
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
and
\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)
are the images of the operators
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{c}
, respectively; therefore,
Eq.

11
implies that
\mathfrak{c}
and
\mathfrak{p}
restrict to a pair of inverse maps
\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)\to\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}
and
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}\to\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
.
These are order isomorphisms, by
Eq.

9
, and thus isomorphisms of complete lattices.
The concept lattice, defined as follows, takes a symmetric approach, incorporating both
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
and
\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}
.

Definition 24

(
[
4
,
5
]
)
.

Let
G\subseteq A\times B
.
The
concept lattice
9
9

9

In formal concept analysis,
A
is a set of
objects
,
B
a set of
properties
, and
G
the relation specifiying which objects have which properties; together, this triple is referred to as a
formal context
.
An element
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in{L_{G}}
is then called a
concept
: it defines a set of objects (
\alpha
) that is uniquely characterised by the set of properties (
\beta
) that they share (i.e.
\alpha=\mathfrak{p}(\beta)
and
\beta=\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)
).

{L_{G}}
is the set

{L_{G}}\coloneqq\{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)\times\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)\mid\alpha=\mathfrak{p}(\beta)\text{ and }\beta=\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)\}

(12)

ordered by

\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\leq\langle\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\rangle\quad:\!\iff\quad\alpha\subseteq\alpha^{\prime}\text{ or, equivalently, }\beta\supseteq\beta^{\prime}.

(13)

For arbitrary
v\in{L_{G}}
, we will denote by
\alpha_{v}\subseteq A
and
\beta_{v}\subseteq B
the closed sets so that
v=\langle\alpha_{v},\beta_{v}\rangle
.

{L_{G}}
forms a complete lattice whose greatest lower bounds and least upper bounds are given by

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}v_{i}=\left\langle\bigcap_{i\in I}\alpha_{v_{i}},\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\beta_{v_{i}}\right)\right\rangle\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigvee_{i\in I}v_{i}=\left\langle\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\alpha_{v_{i}}\right),\bigcap_{i\in I}\beta_{v_{i}}\right\rangle.

(14)

Finally, define the input and output maps

\displaystyle\lambda_{L_{G}}:A\to{L_{G}},\quad a

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\{a\}),\mathfrak{c}(\{a\})\rangle

(15)

\displaystyle\text{ and \hskip 10.22217pt}\mu_{L_{G}}:B\to{L_{G}},\quad b

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\mathfrak{p}(\{b\}),\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{p}(\{b\})\rangle.

(16)

That is,
\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)
is the smallest element
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in{L_{G}}
such that
a\in\alpha
and, dually,
\mu_{L_{G}}(b)
is the greatest element
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in{L_{G}}
so that
b\in\beta
.
The tuple
({L_{G}},\leq,\lambda_{L_{G}},\mu_{L_{G}})
so defined constitutes a circuit, which we will also refer to as the concept lattice (with respect to the relation
G
).

Remark
.

\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
,
\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}
, and
{L_{G}}
defined above provide three different, isomorphic perspectives on the concept lattice.
Denote by
\pi_{A}:{L_{G}}\to\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
the map
v\mapsto\alpha_{v}
; this is an order isomorphism whose inverse is
\alpha\mapsto\langle\alpha,\mathfrak{c}(\alpha)\rangle
.
If we equip
\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)
with the input and output maps
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)}\coloneqq\pi_{A}\circ\lambda_{L_{G}}=\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\{-\})
and
\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{c}(A)}\coloneqq\pi_{A}\circ\mu_{L_{G}}=\mathfrak{c}(\{-\})
, then
\pi_{A}
becomes a circuit isomorphism.
We can do the same to
\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}
using the map
\pi_{B}:{L_{G}}\to\mathcal{P}^{c}(B)^{\text{op}}
defined by
v\mapsto\beta_{v}
, which has inverse
\beta\mapsto\langle\mathfrak{p}(\beta),\beta\rangle
.
The following diagram of circuit isomorphisms then commutes.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad.

Note that in the above example, the inputs in the past of each gate
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
are precisely the elements of
\alpha
, and the outputs in its future the elements of
\beta
.
That is the case in general:

Proposition 26
.

Let
v\in{L_{G}}
.
For any
a\in A,b\in B
, we have

\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)\leq v\iff a\in\alpha_{v}\quad\text{and}\quad v\leq\mu_{{L_{G}}}(b)\iff b\in\beta_{v}.

(17)

In other words, using the notation of
Eq.

2
,
v^{-}=\alpha_{v}
and
v^{+}=\beta_{v}
.
Moreover,

v=\bigvee_{a\in\alpha_{v}}\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)=\bigwedge_{b\in\beta_{v}}\mu_{{L_{G}}}(b).

(18)

Proof.

We have

\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)\leq v\iff\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\{a\})\subseteq\alpha_{v}\iff\{a\}\subseteq\alpha_{v}\iff a\in\alpha_{v}.

The second equivalence here follows from extensitivity (
10
) (left to right) and idempotency (right to left) of the closure operator
\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}
, as well as the fact that
\alpha_{v}
is closed.

As a consequence,
v
is an upper bound to the set
\{\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)\mid a\in\alpha_{v}\}
and must therefore be at least as large as the least upper bound
\bigvee_{a\in\alpha_{v}}\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)
.
The converse statement that
v\leq\bigvee_{a\in\alpha_{v}}\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)
is equivalent to saying that
\alpha_{v}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}\left(\bigcup_{a\in\alpha_{v}}\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}(\{a\})\right)
, which is immediate from extensitivity of
\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{c}
.
The dual statements follow similarly.
∎

Proposition 27
.

Each circuit endomorphism of
{L_{G}}
is trivial:
\End({L_{G}})=\{\id_{{L_{G}}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
f:{L_{G}}\to{L_{G}}
be a circuit morphism.
Since
f
is order-preserving, we have, for all
S\subseteq{L_{G}}
,

f\left(\bigvee S\right)\geq\bigvee f(S)\text{\qquad and \qquad}f\left(\bigwedge S\right)\leq\bigwedge f(S).

Moreover, since
f
is a circuit morphism, it satisfies
f(\lambda_{L_{G}}(a))\geq\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)
and
f(\mu_{L_{G}}(b))\leq\mu_{L_{G}}(b)
for all
a\in A
,
b\in B
.
Combining these facts with
Proposition

26
gives that for
v\in{L_{G}}
,

f(v)=f\left(\bigvee_{a\in\alpha_{v}}\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)\right)\geq\bigvee_{a\in\alpha_{v}}f\left(\lambda_{{L_{G}}}(a)\right)\geq\bigvee_{a\in\alpha_{v}}\lambda_{{L_{G}}}(a)=v

and,
dually,

f(v)=f\left(\bigwedge_{b\in\beta_{v}}\mu_{L_{G}}(b)\right)\leq\bigwedge_{b\in\beta_{v}}f\left(\mu_{{L_{G}}}(b)\right)\leq\bigwedge_{b\in\beta_{v}}\mu_{{L_{G}}}(b)=v.

Therefore
f(v)=v
.
∎

Proposition 28
.

Let
L
be a circuit from
A
to
B
which has connectivity
G
and whose underlying partial order
(L,\leq_{L})
is a complete lattice.
Then
L
admits incoming morphisms
f:P\to L
from any other circuit
P
with connectivity
G_{P}\subseteq G
.

Proof.

Let
P
be a circuit with connectivity
G_{P}\subseteq G
and consider the map

f:P\to L,\quad p\mapsto\bigvee_{a:\lambda_{P}(a)\leq p}\lambda_{L}(a)=\bigvee_{a\in p^{-}}\lambda_{L}(a),

(19)

where
\bigvee
is the join in
L
and
p^{-}
is as in
Eq.

2
.
This map is order-preserving:

p\leq_{P}q\implies p^{-}\subseteq q^{-}\implies\bigvee_{a\in p^{-}}\lambda_{L}(a)\leq\bigvee_{a\in q^{-}}\lambda_{L}(a).

It also respects inputs: for all
a\in A
,

f(\lambda_{P}(a))=\bigvee_{a^{\prime}:\lambda_{P}(a^{\prime})\leq\lambda_{P}(a)}\lambda_{L}(a^{\prime})\geq\lambda_{L}(a).

Finally,
f
respects outputs: since
G_{P}\subseteq G_{L}
, we have
\lambda_{P}(a)\leq\mu_{P}(b)\implies\lambda_{L}(a)\leq\mu_{L}(b)
, so that for all
b\in B
,

f(\mu_{P}(b))=\bigvee_{a:\,\lambda_{P}(a)\leq\mu_{P}(b)}\lambda_{L}(a)\leq\bigvee_{a:\,\lambda_{L}(a)\leq\mu_{L}(b)}\lambda_{L}(a)\leq\mu_{L}(b).

We conclude that
f:P\to L
is a circuit morphism.
A dual construction, which instead sets
f(p)\coloneqq\bigwedge_{b:\mu_{P}(b)\geq p}\mu_{L}(b)
, yields a generally distinct circuit morphism.
∎

The previous three propositions allow us to prove our main result.

Theorem 29
.

Let
A
and
B
be sets and
G\subseteq A\times B
a relation.
The concept lattice
{L_{G}}
is, up to circuit isomorphisms, the unique circuit satisfying the following three properties.

(i)

{L_{G}}
has connectivity
G_{{L_{G}}}=G
.

(ii)

For any circuit
P
with connectivity
G_{P}\subseteq G
, there exists a morphism
f:P\to{L_{G}}
.
11
11

11

Changing the inclusion
G_{P}\subseteq G
in (
ii
) to an equality would not change the validity of the theorem and while it might seem to make the uniqueness statement stronger, it is not difficult to see that this is in fact not the case.

(iii)

If
K
is any other circuit satisfying (
i
) and (
ii
) then there is an injective morphism
{L_{G}}\to K
.

In fact, for any circuit
Q
with connectivity
G
and morphism
g:{L_{G}}\to Q
,
g
is an embedding.

Proof.

The fact that
{L_{G}}
has connectivity
G
is a restatement of a basic result in formal concept analysis
[
5
, Thm. 3]
that follows
directly from
Proposition

26
:

\lambda_{L_{G}}(a)\leq\mu_{L_{G}}(b)\iff a\in\alpha_{\mu_{L_{G}}(b)}\iff a\in\mathfrak{p}(\{b\})\iff aGb.

That (
ii
) is satisfied by
{L_{G}}
is a direct consequence of
Proposition

28
above and the fact that
{L_{G}}
is a complete lattice.

We now prove the final claim in the theorem statement, which implies (
iii
).
Let
Q
be a circuit with connectivity
G
and
g:{L_{G}}\to Q
be a morphism.
By (
ii
), there also exists a morphism
f:Q\to{L_{G}}
.
The composite
f\circ g
is an endomorphism of
{L_{G}}
and is therefore, by
Proposition

27
, the identity morphism
\id_{{L_{G}}}
.
Thus,
g
has a left-inverse
f
which is, in particular, order-preserving; this means that
g
must be an order-embedding and thus a circuit embedding.

It remains to show that
{L_{G}}
is the unique circuit, up to isomorphism, satisfying (
i
)–(
iii
).
Suppose that
K
also satisfies these properties; then there exist morphisms
f:K\to{L_{G}}
and
g:{L_{G}}\to K
such that
f
and
g
are both injective.
The composite
f\circ g:{L_{G}}\to{L_{G}}
is an endomorphism of
{L_{G}}
and therefore, again, equal to the identity
\id_{{L_{G}}}
.
Therefore
f
must be surjective; since it is also injective, it is a bijective circuit morphism whose inverse
g
is also a circuit morphism.
In other words,
f
is a circuit isomorphism.
∎

In terms of the preorder from
Definition

5
—i.e. when forgetting the distinction between parallel morphisms—
Theorem

29
leads to the following.

Corollary 30
.

For any circuit
P
and relation
G\subseteq A\times B
, we have

G_{P}\subseteq G\iff P\preceq{L_{G}}.

(20)

That is, the map
{L_{(-)}}:\mathcal{P}(A\times B)\to\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}
is order-preserving and forms an upper Galois adjoint to the map
G_{(-)}:\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}\to\mathcal{P}(A\times B)
.

This determines the concept lattice up to syntactical equivalence but not up to circuit isomorphism; information about the circuit morphisms (regarding e.g. their injectivity) as in
Theorem

29
is required for the latter.
12
12

12

It is tempting to try to formulate
Theorem

29
itself as the characterisation of
{L_{G}}
as some right adjoint or appropriate colimit in the (non-preorder) category of circuits and circuit morphisms, just as
Corollary

30
does for the preorder
\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}
; however, this seems not possible to do in a meaningful way, as the non-preorder category has too many parallel morphisms.

Remark
.

The Basic Theorem of Formal Concept Analysis
[
4
]
,
[
5
, Thm. 3]
tells us that any complete lattice
(L,\leq)
arises as the concept lattice of
some
binary relation
G
.
More precisely, a circuit
{(L,\leq,\lambda,\mu)}
whose underlying order
(L,\leq)
is a complete lattice is circuit-isomorphic to the concept lattice
L_{G}
iff the connectivity of
L
is
G
and the images
\lambda(A),\mu(B)\subseteq L
are join- and meet-dense in
L
, respectively.

4.2
The basic circuit

To close, we will construct another circuit with a given connectivity relation
G\subseteq A\times B
, denoted by
{B_{G}}
, which generalises the circuit
{B_{C_{3}}}
from
Example

22
.
Dually to
{L_{G}}
, it admits morphisms into (rather than from) all other circuits with connectivity
G
.
There is one obvious candidate for a generalisation of
{B_{C_{3}}}
: it is the circuit
(A\sqcup B,\leq,\iota_{A},\iota_{B})
whose underlying set is the disjoint union
A\sqcup B
; whose order
\leq
satisfies
p<q
iff
p\in A,q\in B
and
pGq
; and whose input and output maps are the inclusion maps
\iota_{A}:A\to A\sqcup B
and
\iota_{B}:B\to A\sqcup B
, respectively.
For a general relation
G
, however, this circuit may contain unnecessarily many gates.
We would like
{B_{G}}
to be the
smallest
circuit with connectivity
G
admitting morphisms into all other circuits with the same connectivity, i.e. satisfying a condition analogous to
Theorem

29
(
iii
).
This requires taking some edge cases into account, leading us to the following definition.

Definition 31
.

Let
G\subseteq A\times B
and define

\displaystyle\bar{A}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{a\in A\mid|G(a)|\neq 1\}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand }

(21)

\displaystyle\bar{B}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{b\in B\mid|G^{-1}(b)|\neq 1\text{ or }\forall a\in G^{-1}(b):|G(a)|=1\}.

(22)

The
basic circuit
with connectivity
G
is the circuit
{B_{G}}=(\bar{A}\sqcup\bar{B},\leq,\lambda_{{B_{G}}},\mu_{{B_{G}}})
, where
\leq
is the partial order satisfying
p<q
iff
p\in\bar{A},q\in\bar{B}
, and
pGq
; and where
\lambda_{{B_{G}}}:A\to\bar{A}\sqcup\bar{B}
and
\mu_{{B_{G}}}:B\to\bar{A}\sqcup\bar{B}
are the maps defined by

\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a)=\begin{cases}a&\text{ if }a\in\bar{A}\\
b&\text{ if }G(a)=\{b\}\end{cases}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt}\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b)=\begin{cases}b&\text{ if }b\in\bar{B}\\
a&\text{ if }G^{-1}(b)=\{a\}\end{cases}.

(23)

Example 32
.

If
A=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{5}\}
,
B=\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4},b_{5}\}
, and

We have the following analogues of
Proposition

27
and
Theorem

29
.
(
Proposition

33
is generally not satisfied by the circuit on
A\sqcup B
discussed above
Definition

31
.)

Proposition 33
.

Each circuit endomorphism of
{B_{G}}
is trivial:
\End({B_{G}})=\{\id_{{B_{G}}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
f:{B_{G}}\to{B_{G}}
be a circuit morphism and let
a\in\bar{A}
.
We show that
f(a)=a
.
First of all, we have
f(a)=f(\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a))\geq\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a)=a
.
Suppose for contradiction that this inequality is strict: that
f(a)>a
.
By construction of
{B_{G}}
, this means that
f(a)=b
for some
b\in G(a)\cap\bar{B}
.
Now, take any
b^{\prime}\in G(a)
.
Then
\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a)\leq\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b^{\prime})
; this is a consequence of the fact that
{B_{G}}
has connectivity
G
, which can be straightforwardly verified.
We therefore have
b=f(a)=f(\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a))\leq f(\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b^{\prime}))\leq\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b^{\prime})
.
Since
b
is by construction a maximal element of
{B_{G}}
, we must in fact have
b=\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b^{\prime})
, which implies
b=b^{\prime}
.
We conclude that
|G(a)|=1
, contradicting the assumption that
a\in\bar{A}
.
Therefore
f(a)=a
.

Next, suppose that
b\in\bar{B}
. We wish to show that
f(b)=b
.
To begin with, we have
f(b)=f(\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b))\leq\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b)=b
.
Suppose that in fact
f(b)<b
.
Then
f(b)=a
for some
a\in G^{-1}(b)\cap\bar{A}
.
Take any
a^{\prime}\in G^{-1}(b)
; we have
\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a^{\prime})\leq\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b)
, so
\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a^{\prime})\leq f(\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a^{\prime}))\leq f(\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b))=f(b)=a
, which by construction means that
a^{\prime}=a
.
Therefore,
|G^{-1}(b)|=1
.
Since
b\in\bar{B}
by assumption, we must have
|G(a)|=1
.
This is in contradiction with the fact that
a\in\bar{A}
.
Therefore
f(b)=b
.
∎

Theorem 34
.

Let
A
and
B
be sets and
G\subseteq A\times B
a relation.
Then
{B_{G}}
is, up to circuit isomorphisms, the unique circuit from
A
to
B
satisfying the following three properties.

(i)

{B_{G}}
has connectivity
G_{{B_{G}}}=G
.

(ii)

For any circuit
P
with connectivity
G_{P}\supseteq G
, there exists a morphism
f:{B_{G}}\to P
.

(iii)

If
Q
is any other circuit satisfying (
i
) and (
ii
) then there is an injective morphism
{B_{G}}\to Q
.

In fact, for any circuit
Q
satisfying (
i
) and (
ii
) and morphism
f:{B_{G}}\to Q
,
f
is an embedding.

Proof.

We only show that (
ii
) holds for
{B_{G}}
.
(
i
) can be straightforwardly verified and all other claims of the theorem follow in a way similar to the proof of
Theorem

29
, now using
Proposition

33
instead of
Proposition

27
.

To show (
ii
), let
P
be a circuit with connectivity
G_{P}\supseteq G
.
Define the map
f:{B_{G}}\to P
by

\displaystyle f(a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lambda_{P}(a)\text{\quad for }a\in\bar{A};

\displaystyle f(b)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mu_{P}(b)\text{\quad for }b\in\bar{B}.

It follows from the construction of
{B_{G}}
and the connectivity of
P
that
f
is order-preserving.

f
also respects inputs: if
a\in\bar{A}
then
f(\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a))=f(a)=\lambda_{P}(a)
, while if
a\in A\setminus\bar{A}
, then
G(a)=\{b\}
for some
b\in\bar{B}
, so that
f(\lambda_{{B_{G}}}(a))=f(b)=\mu_{P}(b)\geq\lambda_{P}(a)
.
The final inequality here is due to the connectivity of
P
.
Similarly,
f(\mu_{{B_{G}}}(b))\leq\mu_{P}(b)
for all
b\in B
.
In conclusion,
f
is a circuit morphism
{B_{G}}\to P
.
∎

Finally, analogously to
Corollary

30
, we can summarise the implications of the above theorem for the preorder on
\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}
as follows.

Corollary 35
.

For any circuit
P
and relation
G\subseteq A\times B
, we have

G\subseteq G_{P}\iff{B_{G}}\preceq P.

(24)

In other words, the map
{B_{(-)}}:\mathcal{P}(A\times B)\to\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}
is order-preserving and forms a lower Galois adjoint to
G_{(-)}:\mathsf{Circ}_{AB}^{\preceq}\to\mathcal{P}(A\times B)
.

Combining
Corollaries

30
and
35
, we see that for any circuit
P
and relation
G
,

G_{P}=G\iff{B_{G}}\preceq P\preceq{L_{G}}.

(25)

5
Closing remarks

Relation to other works

While the treatment of input-output connectivity and the resulting introduction of lattice theory into the study of circuit syntax is, as far as I am aware, new to this work, the combinatorics of circuit syntax more generally has of course been investigated previously.
Most other approaches are based on (directed) graph-like structures, rather than partial orders.

[
14
]
, for example, present a comprehensive formalism for string diagram rewrites based on discrete cospans of directed hypergraphs, establishing a formal equivalence with string diagrams in free symmetric monoidal categories.
Their requirement that rewrites be
convex
seems to parallel the emergence of
compatible
congruences as kernels of circuit morphisms in this work.

Another order-theoretic framework similar to the one here was proposed in recent independent work of
[
20
]
.
Their definitions of circuits and morphisms (there called
framed partial orders
and
frame- and order-preserving maps
) are distinct from, but seem for most practical purposes equivalent to our definitions in
Section

2
.
(Precisely, for any given pair of sets
A,B
the category proposed in
[
20
]
is isomorphic to a strict subcategory of our circuits and circuit morphisms; but the approaches become equivalent after quotienting by syntactical equivalence.)
Like us, they are motivated by considering the (in)existence of morphisms as a means of comparing expressivity of circuit shapes.
The primary focus of
[
20
]
is on the notion of syntactical equivalence and the interaction of circuits with spacetime structure.
In particular, the authors show, restricting to the finite case, that every equivalence class
[P]
contains a circuit
S\in[P]
such that all morphisms
S\to P^{\prime}\in[P]
are embeddings; they call
S
, which is unique up to isomorphisms, the
minimal representative
of the class
[P]
.
This result also holds in the formalism proposed here,
and from
Theorems

29
and
34
we see that
{L_{G}}
and
{B_{G}}
are particular examples of such minimal representatives.

Future directions

The results presented here have relevance to quantum causality.

Theorem

29
shows that the concept lattice acts as a canonical shape for causal decompositions of unitary transformations, studied in
[
6
,
8
,
7
]
.
By doing so it provides the syntax of the circuit decomposition that one should be looking for; what remains is to find appropriate semantics in terms of quantum systems and quantum gates.
This problem boils down to a close interplay between order theory and finite-dimensional operator algebra, exemplified by the characterisation of
[
7
]
.

There also remain interesting questions outside the context of causal decompositions and circuit connectivity.
A natural problem is to characterise the set of finite circuits up to syntactical equivalence for given finite sets
A
,
B
and to study its order- and category-theoretic structure.
A beginning has been made in
[
20
]
, which characterises this set in cases where
A
and
B
have small numbers of elements.
Also the relation between syntactical inequivalence of circuits and their comparative expressivity in specific SMCs such as classical and quantum theory should be investigated in more detail.
